\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\left(\exists n\geq K:\ \ \sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\,D_{\text{\rm KL}}(\widehat{Q}_{n}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))>\zeta(n,\delta)\right)\leq\delta\ .

(48)

Consequently, for any algorithm with an arbitrary sampling rule, stopping time
\tau
given by (
40
) (with the threshold as in (
39
)), and best arm recommendation
\widehat{a}=\arg\max_{a}\widehat{\eta}_{a}(\tau)
we have

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau<\infty,\ \eta_{\widehat{a}}<\eta_{a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})})\leq\delta\ .

(49)

In particular, we note that (
49
) holds for the proposed arms selection rule in (
36
). The next result below shows that the stopping time of policy
Rstl-Dtrack
is finite almost surely, and satisfies an almost-sure asymptotic upper bound that nearly matches with the lower bound in (
27
).

Proposition 6.5
.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
. For all
\delta\in(0,1)
, the stopping time
\tau
of policy
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack}}
is finite almost surely, and hence
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack}}\in\Pi(\delta)
. Furthermore,

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\left(\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\tau}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{\eta\,T_{\text{\rm unif}}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})+(1-\eta)\,T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\right)=1\ ,

(50)

where
T_{\text{\rm unif}}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
in (
50
) is defined as

T_{\text{\rm unif}}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\nu_{\bm{\theta}}^{\text{\rm unif}}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\,D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\ .

(51)

Having established that
Rstl-Dtrack
satisfies the desired error probability constraint, we present below the main result of this section, an upper bound on the growth rate of the expected stopping time of
Rstl-Dtrack
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
. For all
\delta\in(0,1)
, the expected stopping time
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau]
of policy
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack}}
is finite. Furthermore,

\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{\eta\,T_{\text{\rm unif}}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})+(1-\eta)\,T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\ .

(52)

Consequently, letting
\eta\downarrow 0
, we have

\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\ \limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\ .

(53)

Combining Proposition
6.6
with Proposition
4.1
, we see that
1/T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
captures the optimal growth rate of the expected stopping time for BAI in restless bandits with problem instance
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
, i.e.,

\frac{1}{T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\leq\liminf_{\delta\downarrow 0}\,\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{R}(\delta)}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\ \limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack}}}]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\ .

(54)

7
Computational Feasibility of
Rstl-Dtrack

In this section, we present an approach to render
Rstl-Dtrack
computationally feasible.
In particular, we discuss the computation of the infimum in (
38
) at each time step, which can be quite resource-intensive. Due to the presence of arm delays, analytically simplifying this infimum any further is a formidable challenge, as discussed in Section
5
. In this section, we propose an approach to alleviate the computational burden of evaluating the infimum in (
38
),
at the expense of trading off asymptotic optimality. Treating the function
f
that is used to define the single-parameter exponential family of TPMs (see (
1
)) as a heuristic “reward” function for the finite-state MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
, and emulating the techniques in
[
32
]
, we propose a
proxy
for the inner infimum term in the expression (
28
) for
T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
, one that may be easily computed in closed form. For all
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
and
R\in\mathbb{N}
, we show that the proxy term, say
U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
, satisfies
U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})\leq T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
. Furthermore, following the template of Algorithm
1
, and replacing the test statistic
Z(n)
in (
38
) with
U_{R}^{\star}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n))
at any given time
n
, we show that an asymptotic upper bound of
1/U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
may be achieved, thus leading to mismatched lower and upper bounds and, thereby, asymptotic sub-optimality.

7.1
Some Notations

We now introduce some notations. Fix
R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
denote the MDP with state space
\mathbb{S}_{R}
and transition probabilities
Q_{\bm{\theta},R}
defined in (
16
). Fix
\gamma\in(0,1)
and time instant
N>K
. For any policy
\pi
, let

V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\pi}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\gamma^{n}\,f(\bar{X}_{n})\,\bigg|\,(\mathbf{d}(N),\mathbf{i}(N))=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\right],\quad(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\ ,

(55)

where
f:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is the same function appearing in (
1
) and is used to define the single-parameter exponential family of TPMs. In writing (
55
), we treat
f(\bar{X}_{n})
as the heuristic “reward” of the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
at time
n
. Then, (
55
) defines the
value function
associated with policy
\pi
. Furthermore, we note that the expectation in (
55
) is over the randomness induced by policy
\pi
. Because the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
is defined over the finite state space
\mathbb{S}_{R}
, there exists a
deterministic
stationary policy, say
\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}=[\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}):(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}]^{\top}
, such that (see, for instance,
[
13
, Theorem 6.2.10]
)

\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}\in\argmax_{\pi}V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\pi}\ .

(56)

Let
V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}
be the value function of policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}
. Furthermore, denote the optimal
Q
-value function
corresponding to policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}
by

{\rm QV}_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\gamma^{n}\,f(\bar{X}_{n})\,\bigg|\,(\mathbf{d}(N),\mathbf{i}(N),A_{N})=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\right],\quad(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]\ .

(57)

We note that the expectation in (
57
) is over the randomness induced by policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}
. Let

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\coloneqq V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})-{\rm QV}_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\ ,

(58)

and accordingly, define

\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\rm min}\coloneqq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a):\\
a\neq\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\ .

(59)

Subsequently, denote the
span
of
V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}
by

\displaystyle{\rm sp}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star})=\max_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}),(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}|V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})-V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})|\ ,

(60)

Finally, let

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)

\displaystyle\coloneqq{\rm Variance}_{(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\sim Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})),\quad(\mathbf{d}\ ,\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]\ ,

(61)

\displaystyle{\rm Var}_{\rm max}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}{\rm Var}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}))\ .

(62)

7.2
A Proxy for the Inner Infimum in (
28
)

Following
[
32
]
, we propose a proxy for the inner infimum term in (
28
) that is computationally easy to solve, and subsequently use it as a substitute for the test statistic
Z(n)
at any given time
n
. Given any
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
and
\nu\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
, this proxy is defined as

\displaystyle\chi(\nu,\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\max\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a):\\
a\neq\pi_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\end{subarray}}\frac{H_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}{\nu(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}+\frac{H^{\star}_{\bm{\theta},R}}{\min\limits_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\nu(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},\pi^{\star}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}))}\right)^{-1},

(63)

where the terms
H_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
and
H^{\star}_{\bm{\theta},R}
are defined as

\displaystyle H_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\coloneqq\frac{2}{\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{2}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}+\max\left\{\frac{16\,{\rm Var}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}{\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{2}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)},~6\,\left(\frac{{\rm sp}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star})}{\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{2}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}\right)^{4/3}\right\},

(64)

\displaystyle H^{\star}_{\bm{\theta},R}\coloneqq\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)^{2}\,(\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\rm min})^{2}}+\min\left\{\frac{27}{(\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\rm min})^{2}\,(1-\gamma)^{3}},~\max\left\{\frac{16\,{\rm Var}_{\rm max}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star})}{(\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\rm min})^{2}\,(1-\gamma)^{2}},~6\,\left(\frac{{\rm sp}(V_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\star})}{\Delta_{\bm{\theta},R}^{\rm min}\,(1-\gamma)}\right)^{4/3}\right\}\right\}.

(65)

The next result, which is an adaptation of
[
32
, Lemma 1]
, shows that the proxy is a
lower bound
on the inner infimum term in(
28
).

Lemma 7.1
.

(Adaptation of
[
32
, Lemma 1]
)

For all
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
and
\nu\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
, we have
\chi(\nu,\bm{\theta})\leq\psi(\nu,\bm{\theta})
, where
\psi(\nu,\bm{\theta})
is as defined in (
30
).

The proof of Lemma
7.1
follows along similar lines as the proof of
[
32
, Lemma 1]
, and is omitted for brevity.

7.3
A Computationally Efficient Alternative to
Rstl-Dtrack

Using the proxy defined in the previous section, we propose an alternative policy to
Rstl-Dtrack
, which is computationally efficient, by implementing two key modifications to
Rstl-Dtrack
. Before we describe these modifications, we introduce some notations. Given any
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
, let

\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\argsup_{\nu\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})}\chi(\nu,\bm{\theta})

(66)

denote the set of all probability distributions over
\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
that maximise
\chi
over the set
\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
. For any
n\geq K
, denote the vector of empirical state-action frequencies at time
n
by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{N}(n)}{n-K+1}=\left[\frac{N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}{n-K+1}:(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]\right]^{\top}\ .

(67)

Notice that the optimisation problem in (
66
) has a convex objective function (the mapping
\nu\mapsto\chi(\nu,\bm{\theta})
is convex) with a convex set of constraints
\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
, and therefore
\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
may easily be computed using an algorithm such as projected sub-gradient.

The modifications to
Rstl-Dtrack
that lead to a computationally efficient policy are as follows.

1.

In lines 8,9 of Algorithm
1
, we replace
Z(n)
with
\chi\left(\frac{\mathbf{N}(n)}{n-K+1},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n)\right)
.

2.

To implement
\pi_{n}
in line 13, we pick an arbitrary
\nu_{n}^{\star}\in\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n-1))

(
instead of picking
\nu_{n}^{\star}\in\mathcal{W}^{\star}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n-1))
in
Rstl-Dtrack
)
, and use the chosen
\nu_{n}^{\star}
to design
\pi_{n}
according to (
35
).

Except for the above modifications, we retain the other lines of Algorithm
1
as is. We call the modified policy
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
and use
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack-Eff}}
as its shorthand representation.

7.4
Theoretical Guarantees for
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff

In this section, we provide the theoretical guarantees for the proposed
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
policy. To start, we note that the guarantees of Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
hold as is for
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
. The key element in the proofs of the above results is an exploitation of the following property of
\pi_{n}
: for any state-action tuple
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
,

\pi_{n}(a|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\geq\varepsilon_{n}\,\pi^{\rm unif}(a|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\ ,

which holds under
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
. Furthermore, recall that
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
picks an arbitrary
\nu_{n}^{\star}\in\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n-1))
at each time step
n
, and uses this to select arm
A_{n}\sim\pi_{n}(\cdot|\mathbf{d}(n),\mathbf{i}(n))
. In contrast,
Rstl-Dtrack
uses
\mathcal{W}^{\star}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n))
to replace
\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n))
. Hence, an analogue of Lemma
6.3
holds for
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
, with
\mathcal{W}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
replaced with
\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
.

Next, we note that the guarantee in Proposition
6.4
holds as is for
Rstl-Dtrack
. Indeed, from Lemma
7.1
, we note that
\chi\left(\frac{\mathbf{N}(n)}{n-K+1},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n)\right)\leq\psi\left(\frac{\mathbf{N}(n)}{n-K+1},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n)\right)=Z(n)
for all
n\geq K
, and therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\left(\exists n\geq K:\chi\left(\frac{\mathbf{N}(n)}{n-K+1},\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n)\right)\geq\zeta(n,\delta)\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\left(\exists n\geq K:Z(n)\geq\zeta(n,\delta)\right)\ .

(68)

The proof technique in Appendix
J
can be followed to demonstrate that the right-hand of (
68
) is upper bounded by
\delta
, thus establishing the counterpart of Proposition
6.4
for
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
. It then remains to establish the analogues of Proposition
6.5
and Proposition
6.6
for
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
.

Given any
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
, define

U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\sup_{\nu\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})}\chi(\nu,\bm{\theta})\ .

(69)

The next result presents the asymptotic growth rate of the stopping time of
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
.

1.

For all
\delta\in(0,1)
, the stopping time
\tau
of policy
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack-Eff}}
is finite almost surely, and hence
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack-Eff}}\in\Pi(\delta)
. Furthermore,

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\left(\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\tau}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{\eta\,T_{\text{\rm unif}}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})+(1-\eta)\,U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\right)=1\ .

(70)

2.

For all
\delta\in(0,1)
, the expected stopping time
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau]
of policy
\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack-Eff}}
is finite. Furthermore,

\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{\eta\,T_{\text{\rm unif}}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})+(1-\eta)\,U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\ .

(71)

Consequently, in the limit of
\eta\downarrow 0
, we have

\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\ \limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\ .

(72)

Recall that the lower bound in (
27
) is for
all
policies in
\Pi(\delta)
. Combining Proposition
7.2
with Proposition
4.1
, we get

\frac{1}{T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\leq\liminf_{\delta\downarrow 0}\,\inf_{\pi\in\Pi_{R}(\delta)}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\ \limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi^{\textsc{Rstl-Dtrack-Eff}}}]}{\log(1/\delta)}\leq\frac{1}{U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\ .

(73)

The proof of Proposition
7.2
follows along the exact same lines as those of Propositions
6.5
and
6.6
, by replacing
\mathcal{W}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
with
\mathcal{X}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
and
T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
with
U_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
. The full proof is omitted for brevity.

Thus, while the computational feasibility of
Rstl-Dtrack-Eff
is notable, it suffers from asymptotic sub-optimality, as evidenced by (
73
). In contrast,
Rstl-Dtrack
is asymptotically optimal (as evidenced by (
54
)), albeit computationally infeasible. This underscores the fundamental trade-off between computational feasibility and asymptotic optimality, a crucial consideration in pure exploration problems such as BAI.

8
Concluding Remarks and Future Directions

In this paper, we have studied BAI in restless multi-armed bandits under the fixed-confidence regime, when the TPM of each arm belongs to a single-parameter exponential family of TPMs and the arm parameters are unknown. We have shown that the restless nature of the arms gives rise to the notion of arm delays and last observed states, the combination of which constitutes an MDP with a countable state space and a finite action space. By constraining the delay of each arm to be at most
R
for some fixed, positive integer
R
, we have reduced the countable state space to a finite set, making the problem amenable to tractable analysis. Under the above
R
-max-delay constraint, we have obtained a problem instance-dependent lower bound on the limiting growth rate of the expected stopping time (time required to find the best arm) subject to an upper bound on the error probability, in the limit as the error probability vanishes. We have showed that the lower bound is characterized by the solution to a max-min optimization problem in which the outer ‘max’ is over the set of all state-action occupancy measures satisfying (a) the
R
-max-delay constraint, and (b) a natural flow-conservation constraint. The inner ‘min’ is over the set of alternative problem instances. We have devised a policy (
Rstl-Dtrack
) for BAI, based on the idea of D-tracking
[
14
]
, that first estimates the unknown parameters of the arms, and then samples an arm at any given time according to a conditional probability distribution on the arms, conditioned on the values of arm delays and last observed states at that time. As for the stopping rule, we have devised a test statistic whose form is akin to that of the inner ‘min’ expression of the lower bound, but with the true MDP state-action-state transition probabilities replaced with its empirical counterpart. In conjunction with a
random
threshold that is a function of the desired error probability, we have designed a rule for stopping further selection of arms whenever the test statistic exceeds the threshold. We have shown that our policy stops in finite time almost surely, satisfies the desired error probability, and is asymptotically optimal.

The computational complexity of the
Rstl-Dtrack
policy is a notable concern, particularly regarding the computation of the infimum in (
38
) at each time step, which can be quite resource-intensive. Due to the presence of arm delays, simplifying this infimum any further is a formidable challenge, as discussed in Section
5
. One potential approach to alleviating this computational burden is to adopt a technique proposed in
[
21
]
. Their method involves expressing the inner infimum in the lower bound using “projection measures,” which is computationally more tractable, especially for single-parameter exponential families. However, unlike in
[
21
]
, the projection measures in our specific context will depend on
\nu
, the variable of optimization in the outer ‘sup’ expression of the lower bound; this in turn may be attributed to the arm delays in our setting. Resolving this challenge remains an open issue and an intriguing avenue for further research. Additionally, recent studies, such as
[
35
]
, have demonstrated the promise of Thomson sampling-based policies in reducing computational complexity of BAI. It could be worthwhile to explore the extensions to restless settings, which might offer further computational efficiencies and improve the performance of our policy.

Future directions:

While we keep
R
fixed throughout the paper, it is interesting to note that
T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
, the constant appearing in the lower bound, is monotone increasing in
R
and therefore admits a limit as
R\to\infty
; see Remark
3
. It is natural to expect that
\lim_{R\to\infty}T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})=T^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
, where
T^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
is the constant governing the lower bound without the maximum delay constraint. A cursory examination of the analysis in
[
30
, Section XI]
reveals that the above relation indeed holds in the special case when the observations from each arm are i.i.d. However, in the general setting of restless arms, it is unclear whether the above relation holds, and a formal justification of this could be an interesting future direction. While it is natural to expect that
T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
ought to depend on the
mixing times
of the arms, our analysis does not bring out this dependence explicitly. Considering a simple
2
-armed restless bandit problem with
\mathcal{S}=\{0,1\}
in which one arm yields i.i.d. observations according to
\text{\rm Ber}(1/2)
distribution while the other arm is a slowly mixing Markov process, characterizing
T_{R}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
explicitly in terms of the mixing time of the second arm could be an interesting direction to explore. Furthermore, considering a dataset of offline observations from arms with inherent delays, an investigation into how incorporating this offline data affects the overall sample complexity of BAI along the lines of
[
36
]
would be insightful. Finally, we note that the extensions to
hidden Markov
observations from the arms, wherein at each time
n
, the learner observes
\bar{Y}_{n}=u(\bar{X}_{n})
for some known/unknown function
u
, may be of interest. Here, the technical key challenge is that while successive observations
\bar{X}_{n}
and
\bar{X}_{n+1}
from any given arm possess a Markov dependence, the same may not be said about
\bar{Y}_{n}
and
\bar{Y}_{n+1}
. While
[
16
]
considers hidden Markov observations in rested bandits, the lower bound therein does not capture the “hidden” aspect of the observations. It may therefore be worthwhile to first establish a lower bound for BAI with hidden Markov observations in rested bandits and subsequently undertake a formal study of restless hidden Markov bandits.

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
3.1

Fix
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
and
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}),(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}
. Let

M\coloneqq\min\{d\geq 1:P^{d}(j\mid i)>0\quad\forall i,j\in\mathcal{S}\},

(74)

where
P
is the generator of the single-parameter exponential family of TPMs defined in (
2
). From
[
37
, Proposition 1.7]
, we know that
M<+\infty
. Fix
n\geq K
, and suppose that
(\mathbf{d}(n),\mathbf{i}(n))=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
. Let
\pi
denote the uniform arm selection policy that selects the arms uniformly at random at each time instant. We now demonstrate that there exists an integer
N
(possibly depending on
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
and
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
) such that (
15
) holds under
\pi
.
Without loss of generality, let the components of
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
satisfy the ordering
d_{1}^{\prime}>d_{2}^{\prime}>\cdots>d_{K}^{\prime}=1
.
Order the components of
\mathbf{d}
in decreasing order.
Consider the following sequence of arm selections and observations: for a total of
M
time instants, from
n
to
n+M-1
, select the arms in a round-robin fashion in the decreasing order of their component
\mathbf{d}
values. At time
n+M
, select arm
1
and observe state
i_{1}^{\prime}
on it. Thereafter, select arms
2,\ldots,K
in a round-robin fashion in the decreasing order of their component
\mathbf{d}
values until time
n+M+d_{1}^{\prime}-d_{2}^{\prime}-1
. At time
n+M+d_{1}^{\prime}-d_{2}^{\prime}
, select arm
2
and observe state
i_{2}^{\prime}
on it. Continue the round-robin sampling on arms
3,\ldots,K
till time
n+M+d_{1}^{\prime}-d_{3}^{\prime}-1
. At time
n+M+d_{1}^{\prime}-d_{3}^{\prime}
, select arm
3
and observe state
i_{3}^{\prime}
on it. Continue this process till arm
K
is selected at time
n+M+d_{1}^{\prime}-1
and the state
i_{K}^{\prime}
is observed on it.

Clearly, the above sequence of arm selections and observations results in
(\mathbf{d}(n+N),\mathbf{i}(n+N))=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
for
N=M+d_{1}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, using the preceding value for
N
, the probability in (
15
) may be lower bounded by

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{K}\bigg)^{N}\cdot\left[\prod_{a=1}^{K}P_{\theta_{a}}^{M+d_{a}+d_{1}^{\prime}-d_{a}^{\prime}}(i_{a}^{\prime}|i_{a})\right]>0,

(75)

where in (
75
),
(1/K)^{N}
is from the uniform selection of arms, and the strict positivity of the term within square braces follows by noting from (
2
) that for each
\theta\in\Theta
,

P^{d}(j\mid i)>0\implies P_{\theta}^{d}(j\mid i)>0\quad\forall d\geq M,\ i,j\in\mathcal{S}.

This completes the proof.

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
3.3

From
[
30
, Lemma 1]
, we know that
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}=\{Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}):(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}),(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\}
is ergodic (albeit with one modification: the quantity
M
appearing in the proof of
[
30
, Lemma 1]
must be replaced with
M
as defined in (
74
)). Using the preceding result together with the fact that
\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}(a|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})>0
for all
valid

(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
tuples proves the lemma.

Appendix D
Proof of Lemma
3.4

First, we note that

\displaystyle N(n,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=K}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{d}(t)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(t)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{d}(K)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(K)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\}}+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \sum_{t=K+1}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{d}(t-1)=\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}(t-1)=\mathbf{i},A_{t-1}=a,\mathbf{d}(t)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(t)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{d}(K)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(K)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\}}+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \sum_{u=1}^{N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\}},

(76)

where
W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
denotes the next state of the MDP when, for the
u
-th time, the state
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
appeared and arm
a
was chosen subsequently. We then note that for all
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
, the events
\{N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u\}
and
\{W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\}
are independent of one another. Indeed, let
\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
denote the instant at which, for the
u
-th time, the state
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
appeared and arm
a
was chosen subsequently. Then, noting that
\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\leq n-1
almost surely under the event
\{N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\mid N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=K}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}),\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\ \mathbf{i},a)=t\mid N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=K}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\mid\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\ \mathbf{i},a)=t)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\ \mathbf{i},a)=t\mid N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=K}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}((\mathbf{d}(t+1),\mathbf{i}(t+1))=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\mid(\mathbf{d}(n),\mathbf{i}(t),A_{t})=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\ \mathbf{i},a)=t\mid N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=K}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}((\mathbf{d}(t+1),\mathbf{i}(t+1))=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\mid(\mathbf{d}(n),\mathbf{i}(t),A_{t})=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\ \mathbf{i},a)=t\mid N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=K}^{n-1}Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{u}(\mathbf{d},\ \mathbf{i},a)=t\mid N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a),

(77)

where the penultimate line follows from (
13
). On the other hand, it is straightforward to see that
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}))=Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
, which is identical to the right-hand side of (
77
).

Writing (
76
) as

\displaystyle N(n,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{d}(K)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(K)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\}}+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \sum_{u=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{u}(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\}}\,\mathbf{1}_{\{N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u\}},

(78)

taking expectations on both sides of (
78
), and using the monotone convergence theorem, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{d}(K)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(K)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime})+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \sum_{u=1}^{\infty}Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\,\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq u)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{d}(K)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(K)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime})+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\,\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)].

(79)

The desired follows from (
79
) by simply noting that
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathbf{d}(K)=\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}(K)=\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\leq 1
and

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n-1,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]+1

for all
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
.

Appendix E
Proof of Proposition
4.1

We do not provide all the detailed steps here, as most of them follow straightforwardly from
[
30
]
.
Let
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
be fixed. Recall the assumption that each of the arms is selected once at the beginning, from time
n=0
to
n=K-1
. For all
n\geq K
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\Theta^{K}
, let
Z_{\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda}}(n)
denote the log-likelihood ratio (LLR) of all the arm selections and observations from the arms up to time
n
under the instance
\bm{\theta}
versus that under the instance
\bm{\lambda}
. That is,

Z_{\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda}}(n)\coloneqq\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(A_{0:n},\bar{X}_{0:n})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(A_{0:n},\bar{X}_{0:n})},\quad n\geq K.

(80)

It can be easily shown that

\displaystyle Z_{\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda}}(n)=\sum_{a=1}^{K}\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}\ N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,j)\ \log\frac{P_{\theta_{a}}^{d_{a}}(j\mid i_{a})}{P_{\lambda_{a}}^{d_{a}}(j\mid i_{a})}.

(81)

In (
81
),
X_{a-1}^{a}
denotes the sample observed from arm
a
when it is selected for the first time at
n=a-1
, and
N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,j)
denotes the number of times up to time
n
the state
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
appeared, arm
a
was selected subsequently, and state
j\in\mathcal{S}
was observed on arm
a
. Noting that the the action-observation pair
(a,j)
together with
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
defines the next state
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
uniquely, we write

\sum_{j\in\mathcal{S}}\ N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,j)\ \log\frac{P_{\theta_{a}}^{d_{a}}(j\mid i_{a})}{P_{\lambda_{a}}^{d_{a}}(j\mid i_{a})}=\sum_{(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\ \log\frac{Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}{Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)},

(82)

where
N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
denotes the number of times up to time
n
the state
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
appeared, arm
a
was chosen subsequently, and the state
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
resulted. We also note that

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})]=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]\cdot Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\quad\forall n\geq K.

(83)

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
and a policy
\pi\in\Pi_{R}(\delta)
; here,
\Pi_{R}(\delta)
is as defined in (
23
). Assume that
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}]<+\infty
4
4

4

If this condition is not satisfied, then
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}]=+\infty
and the lower bound (
27
) holds trivially.
. Using standard change-of-measure arguments for restless bandits (cf.
[
30
, Appendix A-A]
, it can be shown that

\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[Z_{\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda}}(n)]\geq d(\delta,1-\delta)\quad\forall n\geq K,

(84)

where
d(\delta,1-\delta)
denotes the KL divergence between the distributions
\text{Bernoulli}(\delta)
and
\text{Bernoulli}(1-\delta)
. We note here that (
21
), (
22
), (
83
), and (
84
) hold when
n
is replaced by
\tau_{\pi}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}]<+\infty
. We then have

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[Z_{\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda}}(\tau_{\pi})]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\left[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}\right]+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \sum_{(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(\tau_{\pi},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})]\ \log\frac{Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}{Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\left[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}\right]+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(\tau_{\pi},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]\ D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\bigg[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(\tau_{\pi},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]\ D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\bigg]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\Bigg[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}-K+1])\,\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(\tau_{\pi},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}-K+1]}\ D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\nu\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})}\ \inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\Bigg[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}-K+1])\,\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\nu(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\ D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\Bigg],

(85)

where
(a)
above follows from applying (
83
) to
\tau_{\pi}
, and the last line follows by noting that

\left\{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[N(\tau_{\pi},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}-K+1]}\right\}_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)}

is a probability distribution on
\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
, satisfies the flow conservation property (
21
), the
R
-max-delay constraint (
22
), and therefore an element of
\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
. The above fractional term may hence be upper bounded by the supremum over all elements of
\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
, thereby leading to (
85
). We thus have

\displaystyle\frac{d(\delta,1-\delta)}{\log(1/\delta)}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\nu\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})}\ \inf_{\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})}\Bigg[\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}\left[\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(X_{a-1}^{a})}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\theta}}[\tau_{\pi}-K+1])\,\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\nu(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\ D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a))\Bigg]

(86)

for all
\pi\in\Pi_{R}(\delta)
. Noting that the first term on the right hand side of (
86
) is not a function
\delta
, and using the fact that
d(\delta,1-\delta)/\log(1/\delta)\to 1
as
\delta\downarrow 0
, we arrive at (
27
).

Appendix F
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Fix
(\nu,\bm{\theta})\in\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})\times\Theta^{K}
such that
a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
is unique. Define

u(\nu,\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\coloneqq\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{K}\ \nu(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\,D_{\text{\rm KL}}(Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\|Q_{\bm{\lambda},R}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)).

(87)

Clearly,
u
is continuous in its arguments. From (
8
), it is evident that for each
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
, the set of alternatives
\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})
is non-compact (in fact, an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{K}
). To show that
\psi
in (
30
) is continuous, we employ
[
17
, Theorem 1.2]
, a version of Berge’s maximum theorem
[
38
, p. 84]
for non-compact image sets. For completeness, we restate this result below.

Definition F.1
.

(
\mathbb{K}
-inf compactness)
Let
\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y}
be two topological spaces,
\Phi:\mathbb{X}\to 2^{\mathbb{Y}}
be a mapping from
\mathbb{X}
to the power set of
\mathbb{Y}
, and let
\mathbb{K}(\mathbb{X})
denote the collection of all compact subsets of
\mathbb{X}
. Let

\textsf{Gr}_{\mathbb{X}}(\Phi)\coloneqq\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}:y\in\Phi(x)\}

denote the graph of
\Phi
.
A function
u:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
is called
\mathbb{K}
-inf compact on
\textsf{Gr}_{\mathbb{X}}(\Phi)
if for every compact set
K\in\mathbb{K}(\mathbb{X})
, the lower level set

\{(x,y)\in K\times\mathbb{Y}:y\in\Phi(x),\ u(x,y)\leq\alpha\}

is compact for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma F.2
.

[
17
, Theorem 1.2]

Let
\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y}
be two topological spaces,
\Phi:\mathbb{X}\to 2^{\mathbb{Y}}
be a mapping from
\mathbb{X}
to the power set of
\mathbb{Y}
, and
u:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
be a real-valued mapping on
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}
. Let
\mathbb{K}(\mathbb{X})
denote the collection of all compact subsets of
\mathbb{X}
. Assume that

1.

\mathbb{X}
is compactly generated, i.e., each set
A\subseteq\mathbb{X}
is closed in
\mathbb{X}
if
A\cap K
is closed in
K
for every
K\in\mathbb{K}(\mathbb{X})
.

2.

\Phi
is lower semi-continuous.

3.

u
is
\mathbb{K}
-inf compact and upper semi-continuous on
\textsf{Gr}_{\mathbb{X}}(\Phi)
.

Then, the function
v:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}
defined via
v(x)=\inf_{y\in\Phi(x)}u(x,y)
is continuous.

In the following, we carefully verify that the hypotheses of Lemma
F.2
are satisfied in the context of our setting, with
\mathbb{X}=\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})\times\Theta^{K}
,
\mathbb{Y}=\Theta^{K}
, and
\Phi:\mathbb{X}\to 2^{\mathbb{Y}}
as the mapping
(\nu,\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\mapsto\Phi(\nu,\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})=\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})\subset\mathbb{Y}
.

1.

Verification of hypothesis 1
: The first hypotheses to verify is that
\mathbb{X}
is compactly generated. This hypothesis holds in our work as
\mathbb{X}=\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})\times\Theta^{K}
is locally compact and hence compactly generated
[
39
, Lemma 46.3, p. 283]
.

2.

Verification of hypothesis 2:
The second hypothesis to verify is that
\Phi
is lower-hemicontinuous at
(\nu,\bm{\theta})
. In order to verify this hypothesis, we show a slightly stronger result, namely that
\Phi
is hemicontinuous (or simply continuous) at
(\nu,\bm{\theta})
. Let
(\nu_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})
be a sequence in
\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})\times\Theta^{K}
such that
(\nu_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})\to(\nu,\bm{\theta})
as
n\to\infty
. Let
5
5

5

Any
\varepsilon
such that
\max_{a\neq a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\eta_{a}+\varepsilon<\eta_{a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}-\varepsilon
works.

\varepsilon=\frac{\eta_{a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}-\max_{a\neq a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})}\eta_{a}}{4}.

From Lemma
2.2
, we know that
\dot{A}
is continuous; therefore, there exists
\xi(\varepsilon)
such that for all
\theta,\lambda\in\Theta
satisfying
|\theta-\lambda|<\xi(\varepsilon)
, we have
|\eta_{\theta}-\eta_{\lambda}|<\varepsilon
. Also, there exists
N=N(\varepsilon)
such that for

\forall n\geq N,\quad\|(\nu_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})-(\nu,\bm{\theta})\|<\xi(\varepsilon),\quad\|\bm{\theta}_{n}-\bm{\theta}\|<\xi(\varepsilon).

Because of the choice of
\varepsilon
and the fact that
a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
is unique, it follows that for all
n\geq N
,

\Phi(\nu_{n},\bm{\theta}_{n})=\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta}_{n})=\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})=\Phi(\nu,\bm{\theta}),

thus establishing the continuity of
\Phi
at
(\nu,\bm{\theta})
.

3.

Verification of hypothesis 3
: The third and last hypothesis to verify is that
u:\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
as defined in (
87
) satisfies the following properties:

(a)

u
is upper-semicontinuous on its graph

\textsf{Gr}_{\mathbb{X}}(\Phi)=\{(\nu,\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}:\bm{\lambda}\in\Phi(\nu,\bm{\theta})\}=\{(\nu,\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{Y}:\bm{\lambda}\in\textsc{Alt}(\bm{\theta})\}.

(b)

u
is
\mathbb{K}
-inf compact on its graph
\textsf{Gr}_{\mathbb{X}}(\Phi)
.

Because
u
is continuous, it is also upper-semicontinuous on its graph, and hypothesis 3(a) follows. Hypothesis 3(b) follows from
[
40
, Lemma 2.1(i)]
, noting that
\Phi
is upper-hemicontinuous (a fact that follows from the continuity of
\Phi
established above).

With the hypotheses of Lemma
F.2
now verified, it follows from the preceding result that
\psi
is continuous at
(\nu,\bm{\theta})
. The above arguments may be extended to show that
\psi
is continuous at all
(\nu,\bm{\theta})
such that
a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
is unique.
6
6

6

Recall that the uniqueness of
a^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
was used in the verification of hypothesis 2.

Using the continuity of
\psi
just established, and noting that (a)
\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
is compact for all
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta^{K}
, and (b)
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\Sigma_{R}(\bm{\theta})
is hemicontinuous (i.e., upper-hemicontinuous and lower-hemicontinuous) thanks to the continuity of
\bm{\theta}\mapsto Q_{\bm{\theta},R}
, a simple application of the Berge’s maximum theorem
[
38
, p. 84]
yields that
\bm{\theta}\mapsto T^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
is continuous and that
\bm{\theta}\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{\star}(\bm{\theta})
is upper-hemicontinuous and compact-valued. This completes the proof.

Appendix G
Proof of Lemma
6.1

G.1
Proof of Part 1

The proof below is an adaptation of the one in
[
32
, Appendix D.1]
. To prove (
41
), we consider the event

\mathcal{E}=\bigcup_{(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})}\{\exists M>0\text{ such that }\forall n\geq K,\ \ N(n,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})<M\},

(88)

and demonstrate that
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E})=0
. For each
z^{\prime}=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})
, let

\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}}\coloneqq\{\exists M>0\text{ such that }\forall n\geq K,\ \ N(n,\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})<M\}.

(89)

We shall demonstrate below that
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}})=0
for all
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
. Towards this, let

m_{\bm{\theta}}\coloneqq\max_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}),(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}\min\{r\geq 1:~\exists\pi\;\;\text{\rm such that }\;Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi}^{r}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})>0\}\ .

(90)

A straightforward extension of Lemma
3.1
(without the
R
-max-delay constraint) to include the
R
-max-delay constraint shows that the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
is communicating, i.e., for all
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i}),(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}
, there exists
r\in\mathbb{N}
and a policy
\pi
such that
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi}^{r}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})>0
. In other words, there exists a path of length
r
to reach the state
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
starting from the state
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
. Observe that
r\leq m_{\bm{\theta}}
by the definition of
m_{\bm{\theta}}
. Furthermore, for any
a,a^{\prime}\in[K]
, the state-action pair
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})
may be reached starting from
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
in at most
m_{\bm{\theta}}+1
steps in the following manner:

(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\xrightarrow[\text{select }a]{}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow[\text{shortest path from }(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime\prime})\text{ to }(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})]{}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}).

(91)

In (
91
),
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime\prime})
is an intermediate state that results from selecting arm
a
in state
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})
. Observe that the length of the shortest path from
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime\prime})
to
(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})
is no more than
m_{\bm{\theta}}
. We therefore have

\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}\coloneqq\min_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a),(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})}\ \max_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq m_{\bm{\theta}}+1\\
\text{Policy }\pi\end{subarray}}Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi}^{r}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)>0.

(92)

Next, we note the following result which is a simple consequence of the finiteness of
\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
.

Lemma G.1
.

There exists
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
such that under the uniform policy
\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}
, the state-action pair
(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)
appears infinitely often.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a):(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\text{ appears infinitely often under }\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}\}
. Going further, we fix
z=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathcal{Z}
and
z^{\prime}=(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
arbitrarily.
If
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
, then it is immediate that
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}})=0
. Suppose that
z^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{Z}
. From the communicating property of the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
, we know that there exists
(r^{*},\pi^{*})
(possibly depending on
z,z^{\prime}
) such that
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{*}}^{r^{*}}(z^{\prime}|z)>0
. Note that for all
n\geq K
,

\displaystyle Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}(z^{\prime}|z)

\displaystyle=Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},a^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)

\displaystyle=Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\cdot\pi_{n}(a^{\prime}|\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\cdot(\varepsilon_{n}\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}(a^{\prime}|\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})+(1-\varepsilon_{n})\,\pi_{\widehat{\bm{\theta}}(n-1)}^{\eta}(a^{\prime}|\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\geq Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\cdot\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{K}

\displaystyle\geq Q_{\bm{\theta},R}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}|\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\cdot\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{K}\cdot\pi^{*}(a^{\prime}|\mathbf{d}^{\prime},\mathbf{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{K}\,Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{*}}(z^{\prime}|z),

(93)

where
(a)
above follows from the definition of
\pi_{n}
in (
35
).

Let
\tau_{k}(z)
denote the time instant at which the state-action
z
appears for the
k
th time,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, conditioned on a realisation of
\{\pi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}}\mid\{\pi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty},\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\exists N\text{ s.t. }\forall k\geq N,\ (\mathbf{d}(\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}),\mathbf{i}(\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}),A_{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}})\neq z^{\prime}\mid\{\pi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty},\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\forall k\geq N,\ (\mathbf{d}(\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}),\mathbf{i}(\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}),A_{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}})\neq z^{\prime}\mid\{\pi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty},\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{k=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg((\mathbf{d}(\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}),\mathbf{i}(\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}),A_{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}})\neq z^{\prime}\mid\tau_{k}(z),\,\{\pi_{n}\}_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{k=N}^{\infty}\left[1-\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}}Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}\right)(z,z^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{k=N}^{\infty}\left[1-\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}}\frac{\varepsilon_{n}}{K}\,Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{*}}\right)(z,z^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{k=N}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{r^{*}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+r^{*}}\varepsilon_{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{k=N}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}\varepsilon_{n}\right)\right],

(94)

where
(a)
above follows from the strong Markov property, and the last line follows by noting that
r^{*}\leq m_{\bm{\theta}}+1
and
\varepsilon_{n}<1
for all
n
. Because (
94
) holds for all realisations of
\{\pi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, marginalising across all such realisations, we get

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}}\mid\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}})\leq\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{k=N}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}\varepsilon_{n}\right)\right]\quad\forall z\in\mathcal{Z}.

(95)

This implies that

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}}\mid\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}})\leq\sum_{N_{z}:z\in\mathcal{Z}}\ \prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\ \prod_{k=N_{z}}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}\varepsilon_{n}\right)\right].

(96)

We now note that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a state-action pair
z_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
such that
\tau_{k}(z_{k})\leq k\,S_{R}\,K
, i.e., the state-action pair
z_{k}
is seen
k
times up to time
n=k\,S_{R}\,K
(this can be easily argued via induction on
k
and using the fact that
|\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]|=S_{R}\,K
). For this choice of
\{z_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\ \prod_{k=N_{z}}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}\varepsilon_{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\ \prod_{k=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{z}}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{z}}^{\infty}\ \prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z)+1}^{\tau_{k}(z)+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{z}}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z_{k})+1}^{\tau_{k}(z_{k})+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k=\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}N_{z}}^{\infty}\left[1-\frac{\gamma_{\bm{\theta}}}{K^{m_{\bm{\theta}}}}\left(\prod_{n=k\,S_{R}\,K+1}^{k\,S_{R}\,K+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=0

(97)

for all choices of
\{N_{z}:z\in\mathcal{Z}\}
, where the penultimate line follows by noting that because
n\mapsto n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}
is monotone decreasing, and
\tau_{k}(z_{k})\leq k\,S_{R}\,K
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
by definition, we have

\prod_{n=\tau_{k}(z_{k})+1}^{\tau_{k}(z_{k})+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}~\geq~\prod_{n=k\,S_{R}\,K+1}^{k\,S_{R}\,K+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}\quad\forall k\geq 1.

Thus, (
97
) implies that for all realisations of
\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}}
,

\prod_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}}\mid\{\tau_{k}(z)\}_{z\in\mathcal{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}})=0.

(98)

Marginalising over all such realisations leads to
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E}_{z^{\prime}})=0
for all
z^{\prime}
, which in turn implies that
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\mathcal{E})=0
.

G.2
Proof of Part 2

Our proof is inspired by the proof of Lemma 11 in
[
32
]
, with necessary modifications for the problem studied here. Let
\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})
be as defined in the statement of the lemma; we will soon specify the constant
\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}
appearing in the definition of
\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})
. Let
\mathcal{Z}
and
\{\tau_{k}(z):k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be as defined in Section
G.1
above. In what follows, we prove that when
\varepsilon_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},~\forall k\in\mathbb{N},~\tau_{k}(z)\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})\,k^{4}\bigg)\geq 1-\alpha\quad\forall\alpha\in(0,1).

(99)

The desired result in (
42
) then follows from (
99
) by noting that for every
z=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathcal{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
N(\tau_{k}(z),\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)=k
, and therefore

\bigg\{\tau_{k}(z)\leq\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})\,k^{4}\bigg\}=\bigg\{N(\tau_{k}(z),\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq\left(\frac{\tau_{k}(z)}{\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})}\right)^{1/4}\bigg\}.

We now proceed to prove (
99
). Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Let
g:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be a strictly increasing function with
g(0)=0
and
g(k)\geq k
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\mathcal{H}=\bigg\{\forall z\in\mathcal{Z},~\forall k\in\mathbb{N},~\tau_{k}(z)\leq g(k)\bigg\}.

(100)

We shall prove below that

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})\leq K\,S_{R}\,\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\ \prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\left[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{g(k-1)+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)\cdot j+l}\right],

(101)

where
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
denotes the complement of
\mathcal{H}
, the constant
m_{\bm{\theta}}
is as defined in (
90
) and
\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}
is a constant that depends only on
\bm{\theta}
; see Lemma
G.2
below for a formal definition of
\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}
. We then tune
\varepsilon_{n}
and
g
suitably so that the right-hand side of (
101
) equals
\alpha
. Note that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\ \bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg\{\tau_{k}(z)>g(k)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\ \bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigg\{\tau_{k}(z)>g(k)\text{ and }\forall j\leq k-1,~\tau_{j}(z)\leq g(j)\bigg\},

(102)

whence it follows from an application of the union bound that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{1}(z)>g(1))+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\tau_{k}(z)>g(k)\text{ and }\forall j\leq k-1,~\tau_{j}(z)\leq g(j)\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{1}(z)>g(1))+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\tau_{k}(z)>g(k),~\tau_{k-1}(z)\leq g(k-1)\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{1}(z)>g(1))+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\tau_{k}(z)-\tau_{k-1}(z)>g(k)-g(k-1),~\tau_{k-1}(z)\leq g(k-1)\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\bigg[\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{1}(z)>g(1))+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\ \sum_{n=1}^{g(k-1)}\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\tau_{k}(z)-\tau_{k-1}(z)>g(k)-g(k-1)\mid\tau_{k-1}(z)=n\bigg)\,\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{k-1}(z)=n)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\bigg[a_{1}(z)+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\ \sum_{n=1}^{g(k-1)}a_{k,n}(z)\,\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{k-1}(z)=n)\bigg],

(103)

where

\displaystyle a_{1}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{1}(z)>g(1)),

(104)

\displaystyle a_{k,n}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\tau_{k}(z)-\tau_{k-1}(z)>g(k)-g(k-1)\mid\tau_{k-1}(z)=n\bigg).

(105)

We now upper bound
\alpha_{k,n}(z)
for each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Upper bounding
\alpha_{k,n}(z)
:
Fix
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In order to upper bound
\alpha_{k,n}(z)
, we write the transition kernel
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}
for any
n
as follows:

Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{n}(z)&[Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}(z|z^{\prime}):z^{\prime}\neq z]^{\top}\\
[Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}(z^{\prime}|z):z^{\prime}\neq z]^{\top}&Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}(z|z)\end{pmatrix}.

(106)

In writing (
106
), we assume without loss of generality that the last row and last column of
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}
correspond to state
z
. Given
z^{\prime}\neq z
, let
\mathbf{q}_{n}(z^{\prime},\neg z)\coloneqq[Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime}):z^{\prime\prime}\neq z]^{\top}
. Then, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{k}(z)-\tau_{k-1}(z)>N\mid\tau_{k-1}(z)=n)=\mathbf{q}_{n}(z,\neg z)\cdot\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+N-1}A_{l}(z)\cdot\mathbf{1}.

(107)

In (
107
),
\mathbf{1}
denotes the all-ones vector.

We now note the following result of interest. We continue with the above proof after the proof of the below result.

Lemma G.2
.

Let
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}
be as expressed in (
106
). Let

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}(z^{\prime}|z):z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z},~Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}(z^{\prime}|z)>0\},

(108)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bm{\theta},2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\{Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}^{r}(z^{\prime}|z):z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z},~r\in\{1,\ldots,m_{\bm{\theta}}+1\},~Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}^{r}(z^{\prime}|z)>0\}.

(109)

where
m_{\bm{\theta}}
is as defined in (
90
). Further, let
\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\coloneqq\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta},2}
. Then,

\bigg\|\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}A_{l}(z)\bigg\|_{\infty}\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\,\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{l}\ ,\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

(110)

Proof of Lemma
G.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{S}_{R}\times[K]
, with cardinality
|\mathcal{Z}|=K\,S_{R}
, be enumerated as
\mathcal{Z}=\{1,\ldots,K\,S_{R}-1,z\}
, where
z\in\mathcal{Z}
corresponds to the last row and last column in the expression for
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n}}
in (
106
). In particular, we note that
A_{n}(z)
in (
106
) is a matrix whose rows and columns are indexed by the elements from
\{1,\ldots,K\,S_{R}-1\}
. Given
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, let

r_{z^{\prime}}(n_{1},n_{2})\coloneqq\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{2}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime})

(111)

denote the sum of elements of the matrix
\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{2}}A_{l}(z)
corresponding to the row indexed by
z^{\prime}
. We will now prove that

r_{z^{\prime}}(n,n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{l}\quad\forall z^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K\,S_{R}-1\},~~\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

(112)

The desired result in (
110
) then follows from (
112
) by noting that

\bigg\|\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}A_{l}(z)\bigg\|_{\infty}=\max\bigg\{r_{z^{\prime}}(n,n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2):z^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,K\,S_{R}-1\}\bigg\}.

Fix an arbitrary
z^{*}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}(z|z^{*})\geq\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}
; such a
z^{*}
is guaranteed to exist because the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
is communicating (Lemma
3.2
). Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle r_{z^{*}}(n,n+1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\bigg(A_{n+1}(z)\bigg)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{*})

\displaystyle=1-Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi_{n+1}}(z|z^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\varepsilon_{n+1}\,Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}(z|z^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\varepsilon_{n+1}.

(113)

Similarly, for any
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle r_{z^{*}}(n_{1},n_{1}+n_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\ \sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{*})\cdot\bigg(A_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(z)\bigg)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{*})\cdot\left[\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\bigg(A_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(z)\bigg)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime\prime\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{*})\cdot r_{z^{\prime\prime\prime}}(n_{1}+n_{2}-1,n_{1}+n_{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n_{1}+1}^{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{*})

\displaystyle=r_{z^{*}}(n_{1},n_{1}+n_{2}-1)

\displaystyle=r_{z^{*}}(n_{1},n_{1}+1)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\varepsilon_{n_{1}+1},

(114)

where
(a)
above follows from the fact that
r_{z^{\prime\prime\prime}}(n_{1}+n_{2}-1,n_{1}+n_{2})\leq 1
for all
z^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
, and the last line above follows from (
113
). Along similar lines as above, we note that for all
z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
and
n_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,m_{\bm{\theta}}+1\}
,

\displaystyle r_{z^{\prime}}(n,n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\ \sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{\prime})\cdot\left(\prod_{l=n+n_{1}+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{\prime})\cdot\left[\sum_{z^{\prime\prime}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n+n_{1}+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime}|z^{\prime\prime\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}=1}^{K\,S_{R}-1}\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{\prime})\cdot r_{z^{\prime\prime\prime}}(n+n_{1},n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime})\cdot r_{z^{*}}(n+n_{1},n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)+\sum_{z^{\prime\prime\prime}\neq z^{*}}\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{\prime\prime\prime}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq(1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\varepsilon_{n+n_{1}+1})\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime})+1-\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\varepsilon_{n+n_{1}+1}\,\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\varepsilon_{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\,\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+n_{1}}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime}),

(115)

where the last line follows from the fact that
\varepsilon_{n}
is a decreasing sequence.
By virtue of the fact that the MDP
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\theta},R}
is communicating, there exists
r=r(z^{\prime},z^{*})
such that
Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}^{r}(z^{*}|z^{\prime})\geq\sigma_{\bm{\theta},2}>0
. Here,
r\leq m_{\bm{\theta}}+1
; for a formal justification of this, see Section
G.1
. Using the preceding fact and setting
n_{1}=r
in (
115
), we get

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+r}A_{l}(z)\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+r}\varepsilon_{l}\,Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}\right)(z^{*}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+r}\varepsilon_{l}\right)\cdot Q_{\bm{\theta},\pi^{\text{\rm unif}}}^{r}(z^{*}|z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\left(\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+r}\varepsilon_{l}\right)\cdot\sigma_{\bm{\theta},2}.

(116)

Combining (
115
) and (
116
), we get

\displaystyle r_{z^{\prime}}(n,n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta},1}\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta},2}\,\varepsilon_{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\,\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+r}\,\varepsilon_{l}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\,\varepsilon_{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+1}\,\varepsilon_{l}

\displaystyle=1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\,\varepsilon_{l},

(117)

where the penultimate line follows from using
r\leq m_{\bm{\theta}}+1
.
∎

Plugging
N=g(k)-g(k-1)
in (
107
), and using Lemma
G.2
, we have for all
k\geq 2
that

\displaystyle\alpha_{k,n}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}\bigg(\tau_{k}(z)-\tau_{k-1}(z)>g(k)-g(k-1)\mid\tau_{k-1}(z)=n\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{q}_{n}(z,\neg z)\cdot\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+g(k)-g(k-1)-1}A_{l}(z)\cdot\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\bigg\|\prod_{l=n+1}^{n+g(k)-g(k-1)-1}A_{l}(z)\bigg\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\prod_{l=(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor+1}^{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}A_{n+l}(z)\bigg\|_{\infty}\times\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\ \bigg\|\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}A_{n+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+l}(z)\bigg\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\bigg[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{n+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+l}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\bigg[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{g(k-1)+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+l}\bigg],

(118)

where
(a)
above follows by noting that
\|\mathbf{q}_{n}(z,\neg z)\|_{1}\leq 1
, and the last line follows from noting that
n=\tau_{k-1}(z)\leq k-1\leq g(k-1)
and that
\varepsilon_{n}
is decreasing in
n
. Going forward, let

b_{k}\coloneqq\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\bigg[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{g(k-1)+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+l}\bigg],\quad k\geq 2.

(119)

Along similar lines as above, it can be shown that

\displaystyle a_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\tau_{1}(z)>g(1))

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(1)-g(0)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\bigg[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{g(0)+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+l}\bigg],

(120)

where in writing the last line, we use
g(0)=0
. Let
b_{1}
denote the right-hand side of (
120
).

As the last step in the proof, we tune
g
and
\{\varepsilon_{n}:n\geq 1\}
so that
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}\leq\alpha
.

Tuning
g
and
\{\varepsilon_{n}:n\geq 1\}
:
Noting that
\varepsilon_{n}
is a decreasing sequence, we have

\displaystyle b_{k}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\bigg[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\prod_{l=1}^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\varepsilon_{g(k-1)+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+l}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor-1}\left[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}(\varepsilon_{g(k-1)+(m_{\bm{\theta}}+2)j+m_{\bm{\theta}}+2})^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}(\varepsilon_{g(k)})^{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right]^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{m_{\bm{\theta}}+2}\right\rfloor}

\displaystyle\leq\left[1-\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}(\varepsilon_{g(k)})^{1+S_{R}}\right]^{\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{1+S_{R}}\right\rfloor},

(121)

where the last line follows from noting that
m_{\bm{\theta}}\leq S_{R}-1
.
For
g(k)=\lambda k^{4}
where
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\varepsilon_{n}=n^{-\frac{1}{2(1+S_{R})}}
, we get

\left\lfloor\frac{g(k)-g(k-1)-1}{1+S_{R}}\right\rfloor\geq\frac{\lambda\,k^{3}}{1+S_{R}},\quad(\varepsilon_{g(k)})^{1+S_{R}}=\frac{1}{k^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}.

(122)

Therefore, it follows that for all
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle b_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\left[1-\frac{\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}}{k^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}\right]^{\frac{\lambda k^{3}}{1+S_{R}}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda k^{3}\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}}{(1+S_{R})\,k^{2}\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{k\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\,\sqrt{\lambda}}{1+S_{R}}\right).

(123)

Using (
123
) in (
101
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\leq K\,S_{R}\,\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}

\displaystyle\leq K\,S_{R}\,\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\frac{k\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}\,\sqrt{\lambda}}{1+S_{R}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{K\,S_{R}\,\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}}{1+S_{R}}\right)}{1-\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}\,\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}}{1+S_{R}}\right)}.

(124)

Let
h(\lambda)
denote the quantity on the right-hand side of (
124
). Then, setting

\lambda=\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{(1+S_{R})^{2}}{\sigma_{\bm{\theta}}^{2}}\log^{2}\left(1+\frac{K\,S_{R}}{\alpha}\right),

we get
h(\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta}))=\alpha
. This completes the proof.

Appendix H
Proof of Lemma
6.2

Proof.

Let

M_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{0}=1\}}\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{0}=2\}}\ \cdots\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{0}=K\}}\\
\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{1}=1\}}\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{1}=2\}}\ \cdots\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{1}=K\}}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{n}=1\}}\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{n}=2\}}\ \cdots\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{n}=K\}}\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad E_{n}^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}f(\bar{X}_{0})\\
f(\bar{X}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f(\bar{X}_{n})\end{pmatrix},\quad E_{n}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{\theta_{1}}\\
\eta_{\theta_{2}}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{\theta_{K}}\end{pmatrix}.

(125)

Then, we have

\displaystyle M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}

\displaystyle={\rm diag}(N_{1}(n),\ldots,N_{K}(n)),

(126)

where
{\rm diag}(\cdot)
refers to the operator that converts an ordered set of elements into a diagonal matrix. Note that

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}_{a}(n)-\eta_{\theta_{a}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{a}(n)}\sum_{t=0}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{t}=a\}}\,(f(\bar{X}_{t})-\eta_{\theta_{a}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{a}(n)}\,(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}^{1}-M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}E_{n}^{2})_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{a}(n)}\,(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})_{a},

(127)

where
E_{n}\coloneqq E_{n}^{1}-M_{n}E_{n}^{2}
. Consider the event

\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\bigg\{\forall(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\in\mathbb{V},\quad\forall n\geq K,\quad N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)\geq\left\lceil\frac{n}{\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})}\right\rceil^{1/4}-1\bigg\},

(128)

where
\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})
and
\mathbb{V}
are as defined in Lemma
6.1
. Under
\mathcal{E}
, we note that for all
a\in[K]
,

\displaystyle N_{a}(n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{V}_{R,a}}N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i})\in\mathbb{S}_{R}}N(n,\mathbf{d},\mathbf{i},a)

\displaystyle\geq S_{R}\left(\frac{n^{1/4}}{\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})^{1/4}}-1\right)

\displaystyle\geq c\,n^{1/4}\qquad\forall n\geq n_{0},

(129)

where
n_{0}
and
c
are defined as

n_{0}\coloneqq\inf\left\{n\geq K:n^{1/4}\geq 2\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})^{1/4}\right\},\quad c\coloneqq\frac{S_{R}}{2\lambda_{\alpha}(\bm{\theta})^{1/4}}.

(130)

Eq. (
129
) implies that
M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}
is invertible for all
n\geq n_{0}
under
\mathcal{E}
, in which case it follows from (
127
) that

\widehat{\bm{\eta}}(n)-\bm{\eta}=(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1}M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}\quad\forall n\geq n_{0},

(131)

where
\bm{\eta}\coloneqq[\eta_{\theta_{a}}:a\in[K]]^{\top}
. We then note that under
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\eta}}(n)-\bm{\eta}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1}(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})^{\top}\,(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1}\,((M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1})^{\top}\,(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\min_{a}N_{a}(n)}}\,\sqrt{(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})^{\top}\,(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1}\,(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}\,n^{1/8}}\sqrt{(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})^{\top}\,(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1}\,(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})}

(132)

for all
n\geq n_{0}
, where
||\cdot||_{2}
denotes vector
2
-norm, and
(a)
follows by noting that each entry of
((M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1})^{\top}={\rm diag}\left(N_{1}(n)^{-1},\ldots,N_{K}(n)^{-1}\right)
may be upper bounded by
\frac{1}{\min_{a}N_{a}(n)}
. Because

N_{a}(n)+c\,n^{1/4}<2N_{a}(n)\quad\forall n\geq n_{0},\ \forall a\in[K],

we have
2(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1}\succ(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n})^{-1}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
, where the notation
A\succ M
means that
A-M
is positive definite, and
I_{K}
denotes the
K\times K
identity matrix. Thus, it follows that under
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\bm{\eta}}(n)-\bm{\eta}\|_{2}

\displaystyle<\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{c}\,n^{1/8}}\sqrt{(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})^{\top}\,(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1}\,(M_{n}^{\top}E_{n})}\quad\forall\,n\geq n_{0}.

(133)

Introducing the shorthand notation
\|x\|_{A}=\sqrt{x^{\top}Ax}
for any positive definite matrix
A
, we show below that under
\mathcal{E}
,

\|M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}\|_{(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1}}=o(n^{\beta})\quad\forall\beta\in(0,1/8).

Fix
\beta\in(0,1/8)
arbitrarily. In order to show that
\|M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}\|_{(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{\alpha}I_{K})^{-1}}=o(n^{\beta})
under
\mathcal{E}
, we invoke the following result from
[
20
]
.

Proposition H.1
.

(
[
20
, Proposition 4]
)

Let
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}:n\geq 0\}
be a filtration. Let
\{\mathbf{\eta}_{n}:n\geq 0\}
be a real-valued stochastic process with zero mean and adapted to
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}
(i.e.,
\eta_{n}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n}
-measurable for all
n\geq 0
). Furthermore, suppose that
\mathbb{E}[\exp(x\eta_{n})|\mathcal{F}_{n}]\leq\exp(-x^{2}\sigma^{2}/2)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\geq 0
. Let
(\mathbf{a}_{n}:n\geq 0)
be an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued process adapted to
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
. Let
U
be a positive definite matrix. Then, for all
\delta>0
,

P\left(\|Q_{n}^{\top}R_{n}\|^{2}_{(Q_{n}^{\top}Q_{n}+U)^{-1}}>2\sigma^{2}\,\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\rm det}((Q_{n}^{\top}Q_{n}+U)U^{-1})}}{\delta}\right)\right)\leq\delta,

(134)

where
Q_{n}=[\mathbf{a}_{0}\ \mathbf{a}_{1}\ \cdots\mathbf{a}_{n}]^{\top}
and
R_{n}=[\mathbf{\eta}_{0}\ \mathbf{\eta}_{1}\ \cdots\mathbf{\eta}_{n}]^{\top}
.

Applying Proposition
H.1
with the filtration in (
9
),
d=K
,
U=c\,n^{1/4}I_{K}
,

\mathbf{a}_{t}=[\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{t}=1\}}\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{t}=2\}}\ \cdots\ \mathbf{1}_{\{A_{t}=K\}}]^{\top},\quad\eta_{t}=f(\bar{X}_{t})-\sum_{a=1}^{K}\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{t}=a\}}\,\eta_{\theta_{a}},\quad 0\leq t\leq n,

and
\sigma^{2}=2\,M_{f}
(noting that
|\eta_{t}|\leq M_{f}+\max_{a}\eta_{\theta_{a}}\leq 2\,M_{f}
a.s. for all
t
, where
M_{f}=\max_{i\in\mathcal{S}}f(i)
), we get that for all
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathcal{E}}\bigg(\frac{1}{n^{\beta}}\|M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}\|_{(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4})^{-1}}>\frac{\sqrt{2\,M_{f}}}{n^{\beta}}\,\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\rm det}((M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})(c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1})}}{\delta}\right)}\bigg)\leq\delta,

(135)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathcal{E}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}(\cdot\;|\;\mathcal{E})
.
Noting that under
\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle{\rm det}((M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})(c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1})

\displaystyle=\prod_{a=1}^{K}\frac{N_{a}(n)+c\,n^{1/4}}{c\,n^{1/4}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{a=1}^{K}\frac{n+1+c\,n^{1/4}}{c\,n^{1/4}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n^{3/4}}{c}+1+\frac{1}{c\,n^{1/4}}\right)^{K}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2n^{3/4}}{c}\right)^{K}

(136)

for all
n\geq n_{1}
, where

n_{1}\coloneqq\inf\left\{n\geq n_{0}:1+\frac{1}{c\,n^{1/4}}\leq\frac{n^{3/4}}{c}\right\}.

(137)

We then have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathcal{E}}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\beta}}\|M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}\|_{(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1}}>\frac{\sqrt{2\,M_{f}}}{n^{\beta}}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2^{K/2}\,n^{3K/8}}{c^{K/2}\,\delta}\right)}\right)\leq\delta\quad\forall n\geq n_{1}.

(138)

Equivalently, for all
\xi>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathcal{E}}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\beta}}\|M_{n}^{\top}E_{n}\|_{(M_{n}^{\top}M_{n}+c\,n^{1/4}I_{K})^{-1}}>\xi\right)\leq\frac{2^{K/2}\,n^{3K/8}}{c^{K/2}}\ \exp\left(-\frac{\xi^{2}\,n^{2\beta}}{2\,M_{f}}\right)\quad\forall n\geq n_{1}.

(139)

Using (
139
) in (
133
), we get that for any
\xi>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\mathcal{E}}(\|\widehat{\bm{\eta}}(n)-\bm{\eta}\|>\xi)\leq\frac{2^{K/2}\,n^{3K/8}}{c^{K/2}}\ \exp\left(-\frac{c\,\xi^{2}\,n^{1/4}}{2(2\,M_{f})}\right)\quad\forall n\geq n_{1}.

(140)

Finally, fixing
N\geq K
and applying (
140
) to the event
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{N}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{N}
is as defined in (
224
), and denoting the constants
n_{0}
,
c
, and
n_{1}
more explicitly as
n_{0}(N)
,
c_{N}
, and
n_{1}(N)
7
7

7

We assume, without loss of generality, that
